I updated the list of OpenAI API alternatives for LLM Zoomcamp students.

The list includes providers that offer free rate-limited access to their models.

It's needed for the course, but anyone can use it.

Two main categories:

1. Free to use (ongoing free tier, no payment required)

Google Gemini, Groq, Mistral AI, OpenRouter, Cerebras, SambaNova Cloud, GitHub Models, Cloudflare Workers AI, and others.

2. Free signup credits, then paid

Anthropic, xAI Grok, DeepSeek, AWS Bedrock, NVIDIA NIM, and others.

The list was reviewed on June 30, 2026. Free tiers change often, so double-check current limits before relying on them.

Full list: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp/blob/main/awesome-llms.md#openai-api-alternatives

Sprint 2 of AI Shipping Labs starts today.

You can join to work on your personal plan or/and participate in the LLM Zoomcamp cohort for AI Shipping Labs members.

So you can use the sprint in two ways:

- Follow your personal plan
- Follow your personal plan and go through LLM Zoomcamp with extra structure

1. Personal plan

When you join the community, we help you create a plan based on your background, goals, and current situation.

During the sprint, you use this plan to work on it week by week, share progress, and get feedback from other members.

2. LLM Zoomcamp

LLM Zoomcamp is a free course where you learn how to build an AI system that answers questions about your knowledge base.

The course materials are free. But for people who want more structure while going through the course, we also have an optional paid option: AI Shipping Labs.

What you get:

- Regular office hours on Zoom, with recordings if you can't join live
- Accountability sprints where you share progress and stay consistent
- Personalized onboarding to set goals and build a learning plan
- A group of other members going through the course together

3. AI Shipping Labs membership

Apart from sprints, AI Shipping Labs membership also includes hands-on workshops, group coding sessions, and a closed community of AI engineering practitioners.

Sprint 2: https://aishippinglabs.com/events/series/2/july-2026-community-sprint

- Context engineering
- Kestra setup
- AI Copilot

- Multi-agent systems
- Cost, security, observability, and production readiness

## 2026-06-25T07:00:02.549Z

- ID: `9549131`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2070039294669213734

A RAG application is usually more than one Python function.

Even a simple system can quickly become a workflow:

- Ingest documents
- Clean and chunk text
- Create embeddings
- Store them in a vector database
- Retrieve relevant context
- Call an LLM
- Evaluate the answer
- Monitor feedback and failures

When everything is in one script, it is fine for learning.

When you want to run it repeatedly, debug failures, and understand what happened yesterday, orchestration becomes useful.

That is why we added a new orchestration module to LLM Zoomcamp this year.

We use @kestra_io for it, and the module covers AI, RAG, and agentic workflows, as well as observability, cost, security, and production readiness.

The course is free. You can find the module materials here: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp/tree/main/03-orchestration

LLM Zoomcamp has a new module this year: AI orchestration with Kestra.

In previous editions, we covered the core parts of building LLM applications:

- RAG
- Vector search
- Evaluation
- Monitoring
- Best practices
- End-to-end project work

This year, we added a dedicated module on orchestration.

This is an important missing piece when moving from a notebook or prototype to a real workflow. You need to run steps in order, pass state between them, handle retries, track failures, and make the system easier to observe.

The module is taught by @wrussell1999 from @kestra_io.

It's a 10-week online course where you learn how to build an AI system that answers questions about your knowledge base.

The course materials, videos, homework, and community support from DataTalksClub are free.

For people who want more structure while going through the course, we also have an optional paid option: AI Shipping Labs.

This is for learners who want extra support beyond the free course, and it’s also a way to support LLM Zoomcamp.

- Regular office hours on Zoom, with recordings if you can't join live
- Accountability sprints where you share progress and stay consistent
- Personalized onboarding to set goals and build a learning plan

## 2026-06-24T07:00:15.345Z

- ID: `9333135`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2069676961367253448

With function calling, the LLM does not run your Python function.

What happens is a protocol:

1. You describe the tool
2. The model decides to use it
3. The model returns a structured tool call
4. Your code parses the arguments
5. Your code runs the function
6. Your code sends the result back to the model
7. The model uses the result to answer

The model can choose:

- Which tool name to call
- Which arguments to pass
- Whether another tool call is needed

But your application is still responsible for executing the function and deciding what to trust.

In my workshop on going from RAG to agents, I showed how tool calling provides the model with a structured way to request actions.

But it's important to remember that it does not remove the need for application logic, validation, logging, retries, or guardrails.

Recording: https://youtube.com/live/RAqLWJsLZb4

Want to learn how to build RAG and agents? Join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-17T17:00:16.258Z

- ID: `9333173`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2067291245144429032

Most agent frameworks are wrapping the same basic loop:

Call the model.
If the response contains tool calls, execute them.
Append the tool results to the message history.
Call the model again.
Repeat until the response has no tool calls.

Frameworks add useful things around this:

- Tool registration
- Schema generation
- Message history handling
- Callback hooks
- Debug views
- Cost tracking
- Error handling

But the core loop is still small enough to understand.

In my workshop about going from RAG to agents, we implemented it manually first:

- Start with instructions and a user question
- Send them to the model
- Check every response item
- If it is a function call, run the function
- Add both the tool call and output to the history
- Stop when there are no function calls left

Once you understand this, agent frameworks become easier to evaluate.

The framework matters. The mental model matters more.

Recording: https://youtube.com/live/RAqLWJsLZb4

Want to learn how to build RAG and agents? Join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-02T09:21:43.902Z

- ID: `9341652`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9341652&a=188312

Retrieval settings often start as gut feeling.

In the workshop, I had one too.

For FAQ search, I assumed the `question` field should be boosted more than the `answer` field.

The reasoning sounded plausible:

If the user asks a question, matching the FAQ question should matter more than matching random words in the answer.

Then we evaluated it.

We generated a ground-truth set, ran search for each question, computed hit rate and MRR, and tried different boost values.

The result did not fully match my initial assumption.

Boosting the question field too much made the ranking worse. The better settings gave more relative weight to the answer text and less to the section field.

This is why evaluation is useful.

It turns retrieval tuning from opinion into an experiment.

You can compare:

- Field boost values
- Top K values
- Text search vs vector search
- Hybrid search variants
- Different embedding models
- Rerankers

For small datasets, grid search is enough.

For expensive evaluations, use smarter optimization.

The important part is not the specific boost value. The important part is having a repeatable way to check whether a retrieval change improved the metric that matters.

Watch my latest workshop on evaluating RAG and agents to learn how to do it: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WUGtDveIe7A

Or join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course about building LLM-based applications: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-02T09:21:20.575Z

- ID: `9341648`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9341648&a=188312

Hit rate and MRR answer two different retrieval questions.

Hit rate asks:

"Did the expected document appear anywhere in the top results?"

If the right document is in the top 5, it counts as a hit.

This is useful, but it ignores position.

The expected document in position 1 and position 5 both count the same.

MRR, mean reciprocal rank, adds position.

If the expected document is:

- Rank 1: score 1.0
- Rank 2: score 0.5
- Rank 3: score 0.33
- Rank 4: score 0.25
- Not found: score 0

Then we average this across all evaluation questions.

For RAG, this matters because top context is usually more useful than buried context.

An LLM may still recover the answer if the right chunk is third or fifth, especially when top K is small.

But higher-ranked relevant context is easier to use, cheaper to inspect, and less likely to be diluted by irrelevant text.

So I usually look at both:

- Hit rate: are we finding the answer?
- MRR: are we ranking it well?

The first tells you coverage.

The second tells you retrieval quality.

Watch my latest workshop on evaluating RAG and agents to learn how to do it: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WUGtDveIe7A

Or join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course about building LLM-based applications: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

Hit rate and MRR answer two different retrieval questions.

Hit rate asks:

"Did the expected document appear anywhere in the top results?"

If the right document is in the top 5, it counts as a hit.

This is useful, but it ignores position.

The expected document in position 1 and position 5 both count the same.

MRR, mean reciprocal rank, adds position.

If the expected document is:

- Rank 1: score 1.0
- Rank 2: score 0.5
- Rank 3: score 0.33
- Rank 4: score 0.25
- Not found: score 0

Then we average this across all evaluation questions.

For RAG, this matters because top context is usually more useful than buried context.

An LLM may still recover the answer if the right chunk is third or fifth, especially when top K is small.

But higher-ranked relevant context is easier to use, cheaper to inspect, and less likely to be diluted by irrelevant text.

So I usually look at both:

- Hit rate: are we finding the answer?
- MRR: are we ranking it well?

The first tells you coverage.

The second tells you retrieval quality.

## 2026-06-02T09:19:05.826Z

- ID: `9341637`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9341637&a=188312

A small detail makes RAG evaluation much easier:

Give every source record a stable ID.

When you generate evaluation questions, keep the ID of the record that produced the question.

Then retrieval evaluation becomes straightforward:

1. Run search with the generated question
2. Get the top K documents
3. Compare their IDs with the expected ID

Without IDs, you end up comparing text.

That gets messy quickly:

- Answers may be paraphrased
- Similar FAQ records may overlap
- Formatting may change
- Duplicates may appear
- Document chunks may be regenerated

IDs give you a clean join key between:

- The original knowledge base
- The generated ground-truth questions
- The retrieved search results
- The evaluation records

In the workshop, I also used structured outputs when generating questions.

That matters for automation.

If the LLM returns a predictable object, you can store it, validate it, and run metrics without manually cleaning text every time.

For RAG evals, stable IDs plus structured outputs are not fancy. They are plumbing.

But good plumbing is what lets you measure reliably.

Watch my latest workshop on evaluating RAG and agents to learn how to do it: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WUGtDveIe7A

Or join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course about building LLM-based applications: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-02T09:18:42.507Z

- ID: `9341634`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9341634&a=188312

When you build a new RAG assistant, you often do not have real user logs yet.

But you still need an evaluation set.

One practical starting point is synthetic data.

Take a known FAQ record:

- Question
- Answer
- Document ID

Then ask an LLM to generate several realistic user questions that could lead to that answer.

Now you have pairs like:

- Generated user question
- Expected document ID

This is enough to start evaluating retrieval.

For each generated question, run search and check whether the expected document appears in the top results.

This gives you a first version of:

- Hit rate
- MRR
- Failed query examples
- Retrieval parameter comparisons

There is an important caveat.

Synthetic questions are usually too clean. They may resemble the FAQ text more than real user behavior.

So I treat them as a bootstrap, not as the final evaluation.

The next step is collecting real user questions, labeling them, and adjusting the synthetic generator so it better matches production data.

But synthetic evals are still much better than changing retrieval settings by gut feeling.

Watch my latest workshop on evaluating RAG and agents to learn how to do it: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WUGtDveIe7A

Or join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course about building LLM-based applications: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-02T09:23:12.826Z

- ID: `9341660`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9341660&a=188312

Exact string matching is usually the wrong way to evaluate RAG answers.

The generated answer can use different words and still be correct.

That is why LLM-as-judge is useful.

Give the judge:

- The user question
- The reference answer
- The answer generated by the RAG system

Ask whether the generated answer is semantically equivalent to the reference answer.

In the workshop, I used structured output with:

- A verdict: good or bad
- Reasoning: why the judge made that decision

The reasoning is not mainly for the user. It is for debugging and calibration.

If the judge marks a bad answer as good, the evaluation score becomes misleading.

So the judge needs its own evaluation loop:

1. Sample judged examples
2. Manually inspect them
3. Check whether the judge is too strict or too lenient
4. Adjust the prompt
5. Rerun and compare

Automated judging is useful.

But it is not a replacement for human judgment. It is a way to scale evaluation after you have checked that the judge behaves reasonably.

Watch my latest workshop on evaluating RAG and agents to learn how to do it: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WUGtDveIe7A

Or join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course about building LLM-based applications: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-02T09:23:34.194Z

- ID: `9341666`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9341666&a=188312

For a plain RAG pipeline, we can evaluate the final answer against a reference answer.

For agents, that is not enough.

An agent can reach a decent final answer through a bad path.

For example:

- It calls search too many times
- It rewrites the query in a harmful way
- It ignores a useful tool result
- It uses the wrong tool first
- It spends unnecessary tokens and latency

So agent evaluation should include the trajectory.

The trajectory is the sequence of actions the agent took:

- Tool calls
- Tool arguments
- Tool outputs
- Intermediate decisions
- Final answer

Then the judge can evaluate two things:

1. Was the final answer correct?
2. Was the tool-use path reasonable?

This matters because production failures are not only wrong answers.

They are also slow answers, expensive answers, unstable loops, unnecessary retries, and actions that should not have happened.

For agentic RAG, I would store the trajectory by default.

If you cannot inspect the path, you cannot reliably debug the agent.

Watch my latest workshop on evaluating RAG and agents to learn how to do it: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WUGtDveIe7A

Or join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course about building LLM-based applications: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-02T09:17:40.422Z

- ID: `9341626`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9341626&a=188312

A RAG system has several places where things can fail.

The answer can be bad because the model generated poorly. But often the answer is bad earlier than that: retrieval didn't find the right context.

That is why I like evaluating search separately before evaluating the full RAG pipeline.

For a basic RAG assistant, the flow is:

1. User asks a question
2. Search retrieves candidate documents
3. The prompt includes those documents
4. The LLM generates an answer

If step 2 is weak, the rest of the pipeline is already in trouble.

Watch my latest workshop on evaluating RAG and agents to learn how to do it: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WUGtDveIe7A

Or join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course about building LLM-based applications: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-01T16:23:21.445Z

- ID: `9333150`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9333150&a=188312

With function calling, the LLM does not run your Python function.

What happens is a protocol:

1. You describe the tool
2. The model decides to use it
3. The model returns a structured tool call
4. Your code parses the arguments
5. Your code runs the function
6. Your code sends the result back to the model
7. The model uses the result to answer

The model can choose:

- Which tool name to call
- Which arguments to pass
- Whether another tool call is needed

But your application is still responsible for executing the function and deciding what to trust.

In my workshop on going from RAG to agents, I showed how tool calling provides the model with a structured way to request actions.

But it's important to remember that it does not remove the need for application logic, validation, logging, retries, or guardrails.

Recording: https://youtube.com/live/RAqLWJsLZb4

## 2026-06-04T07:00:05.325Z

- ID: `9174968`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2062429161747296505

Your first RAG implementation should be easy to replace.

In my recent workshop, we started with a modular setup:

1. Search
2. Build prompt
3. Call LLM

Then we moved the logic into reusable components.

It's important because the first version is rarely the final version.

You may start with:

- MinSearch and later move to SQLite search, Elasticsearch, Postgres, or vector search.
- OpenAI and later try Anthropic, Gemini, or a local model.
- A notebook and later move the logic into a service.

If everything is mixed together, every change becomes painful.

A better pattern is to keep dependencies explicit: the search index, the LLM client, the prompt template, the course filter, and the model name.

Then replacing one part does not require rewriting the whole assistant.

Check out my workshop recording to build your first RAG application: https://www.youtube.com/live/KSItlTAsMsk?si=xQDsAgtnzB9wAC4-

Want to go from LLM basics to a production-ready AI assistant in 10 weeks? Join my free LLM Zoomcamp that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-10T07:00:15.348Z

- ID: `9333232`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2064603531261468957

Hard truth: You don't always need an agent.

Sometimes you need parsing, retrieval, or a deterministic workflow with a few LLM calls.

An agent becomes useful when the system needs to decide what action to take next: which tool to call, with which arguments, in which order, and whether another attempt is needed.

But that flexibility is not free.

When you add an agent, you usually introduce more model calls, latency, traces to inspect, failure modes, and opportunities for the system to behave unexpectedly.

My default approach is:

1. Start with the simplest workflow
2. Measure where it fails
3. Add tools where the model needs external information or actions
4. Add an agentic loop only when fixed control flow is too rigid
5. Monitor cost, traces, and failure modes from the start

Watch this workshop recording to learn more about my approach: https://youtube.com/live/RAqLWJsLZb4

Want to learn how to build RAG and agents? Join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-01T16:24:14.613Z

- ID: `9333160`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9333160&a=188312

Most agent frameworks are wrapping the same basic loop:

Call the model.
If the response contains tool calls, execute them.
Append the tool results to the message history.
Call the model again.
Repeat until the response has no tool calls.

Frameworks add useful things around this:

- Tool registration
- Schema generation
- Message history handling
- Callback hooks
- Debug views
- Cost tracking
- Error handling

But the core loop is still small enough to understand.

In my workshop about going from RAG to agents, we implemented it manually first:

- Start with instructions and a user question
- Send them to the model
- Check every response item
- If it is a function call, run the function
- Add both the tool call and output to the history
- Stop when there are no function calls left

Once you understand this, agent frameworks become easier to evaluate.

The framework matters. The mental model matters more.

Recording: https://youtube.com/live/RAqLWJsLZb4

Want to learn how to build RAG and agents? Join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-08T07:00:17.086Z

- ID: `9333190`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2063878762744402140

When people talk about "agent memory", it can sound abstract.

In the simplest useful agent, memory is just the message history.

For a tool-using agent, that history usually includes:

- The developer instructions
- The user message
- The model's tool call
- The tool output
- The next model message
- Any follow-up user messages

This is why traces are so useful.

If the final answer is wrong, you can inspect and debug the chain:

Did the model choose the right tool?
Did it pass the right arguments?
Did the tool return the expected result?
Was the result added back to the conversation correctly?

Most agent bugs become much easier to understand once you treat memory as explicit state, not as something mysterious inside the model.

Recording: https://youtube.com/live/RAqLWJsLZb4

Hard truth: You don't always need an agent.

Sometimes you need parsing, retrieval, or a deterministic workflow with a few LLM calls.

An agent becomes useful when the system needs to decide what action to take next: which tool to call, with which arguments, in which order, and whether another attempt is needed.

But that flexibility is not free.

When you add an agent, you usually introduce more model calls, latency, traces to inspect, failure modes, and opportunities for the system to behave unexpectedly.

My default approach is:

1. Start with the simplest workflow
2. Measure where it fails
3. Add tools where the model needs external information or actions
4. Add an agentic loop only when fixed control flow is too rigid
5. Monitor cost, traces, and failure modes from the start

Watch this workshop recording to learn more about my approach: https://youtube.com/live/RAqLWJsLZb4

Want to learn how to build RAG and agents? Join LLM Zoomcamp, our free course that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-06-15T07:00:02.144Z

- ID: `9332848`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2066415415149142474

A plain RAG system usually follows a fixed path:

1. Take the user question
2. Search the knowledge base
3. Put the results into a prompt
4. Ask the LLM to answer

This works well when the query is clean and the retrieval step finds the right context.

It gets weaker when the query is messy.

In the workshop, I used a small example: a student asks how to run Ollama locally, but types it in a way that lexical search does not match well. In a fixed RAG flow, we already committed to the search query before the LLM sees the results.

With agentic RAG, the flow changes.

Instead of forcing search first, we tell the LLM:

"Here is the user's question. Here is a search tool you can use."

Then the LLM can decide:

- Whether search is needed
- What query to send
- Whether to rewrite the query
- Whether another search is needed

This is the important shift.

Agentic RAG is not magic. It is still retrieval plus generation. But the retrieval step becomes something the model can control through tool calls.

That makes the system more flexible, especially when the user's input is incomplete, misspelled, or phrased differently from the documents.

It also makes the system more complex. Now you have more calls, more state, and more things to observe.

So I would not start every RAG project with an agent. I would start with the fixed flow, measure where it fails, and add agentic behavior only where the system needs that extra flexibility.

Recording: https://youtube.com/live/RAqLWJsLZb4

## 2026-05-22T17:00:36.267Z

- ID: `9176590`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2057869244902322225

Don't start a RAG project with vector search by default.

Start with a text search.

It is simpler:

- No embedding model
- No vector database
- No extra query-time encoding step
- Fewer dependencies
- Easier debugging

Vector search is useful when users ask the same thing with different words.

For example:

"Can I still join the course?" or "Can I still enroll in the program?"

These are semantically close, but a lexical search may miss the connection if the words don't overlap.

That's where embeddings help, but they also add overhead.

You need to encode the dataset, store the vectors, encode each query, choose a search backend, and evaluate whether the improvement is worth it.

My rule:

1. Build version 1 with text search
2. Then evaluate
- If vector search gives a meaningful improvement, add it.
- If the improvement is marginal, the complexity may not be worth it.

-> Use my workshop recording to learn how vector search fits into RAG: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BC3NsRUNEIg&t=714s

Want to go from LLM basics to a production-ready AI assistant in 10 weeks? Join my free LLM Zoomcamp that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-05-26T07:00:14.604Z

- ID: `9191342`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2059167710274334974

Once an LLM application is deployed, the work is not finished.

Now you need to understand what happens when real users start using it:

- What questions do they ask?
- Which documents are retrieved?
- What context is passed to the model?
- What answer is generated?
- Was the answer useful?
- Where does the pipeline fail?

In the next LLM Zoomcamp workshop, we'll focus on monitoring.

📅 Wednesday, June 3, 2026
🕛 12:00-13:30 CET
📍 YouTube

We'll go through a hands-on example and look at how to:

- Collect traces from an LLM pipeline
- Instrument LLM calls and retrieval steps
- Store chat history and user interactions
- Collect user feedback on generated answers
- Track answer quality over time
- Use dashboards to inspect system behavior
- Run evaluations on production traces

Before deployment, we evaluate on test data. After deployment, we use traces, feedback, and production metrics to understand what actually happens in the system. This helps us detect issues in retrieval, generation, latency, cost, and answer quality.

Register here: https://luma.com/w708z26f

## 2026-05-28T14:00:15.543Z

- ID: `9176599`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2059998186639675555

Vector search adds a second lifecycle to your RAG application.

With text search, indexing is almost instant for small datasets.

With vector search, you need to generate embeddings for every document. That takes time.

You don't want this to happen every time the application starts.

A better structure:

1. Ingestion process

Load documents, generate embeddings, store them in a database.

2. Application process

Load the existing index and answer user queries.

This is why persistence matters.

For a demo, everything can live in one notebook. But for an application, ingestion and serving should be separate.

Check out my workshop recording to learn how vector search fits into RAG: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BC3NsRUNEIg&t=714s

## 2026-05-26T14:02:11.211Z

- ID: `9176596`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2059273896105595297

Why retrieve top 5 documents instead of only top 1 in RAG?

Because the answer isn't always in one document.

Sometimes:

- One document has the direct answer
- Another adds an important condition
- The top result is close but not sufficient
- The second or third result is actually more useful

Then the LLM can use the retrieved context to produce a better answer

Top 5 is not a magic number; you can choose this number based on your dataset.

You choose it and evaluate the results to make a final decision:

- Maybe top 3 is enough.
- Maybe top 10 improves recall.
- Maybe too many documents add noise.

The important part here is to treat retrieval as something you measure and evaluate.

-> Use my workshop recording to learn how vector search fits into RAG: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BC3NsRUNEIg&t=714s

## 2026-05-27T14:00:37.743Z

- ID: `9164504`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2059635888133640365

A basic RAG application is just three steps.

1. Search: Find documents that are likely to contain the answer.

2. Build prompt: Turn the user question and search results into context the model can use.

3. Call the LLM: Generate the final answer.

That’s enough for the first version.

In my recent workshop, we built this with a course FAQ assistant. A student asks a question, search returns relevant FAQ entries, we build a prompt with the question and context, and the LLM answers.

The useful part of this structure is that every step is visible.

You can inspect search results.
You can print the prompt.
You can change the model.
You can improve retrieval without touching the LLM call.

Before adding frameworks, agents, or orchestration, make sure these three functions work.

A simple RAG pipeline is much easier to debug when you can see each step.

Check out my workshop recording to build your first RAG application: https://www.youtube.com/live/KSItlTAsMsk?si=xQDsAgtnzB9wAC4-

Want to go from LLM basics to a production-ready AI assistant in 10 weeks? Join my free LLM Zoomcamp that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9190588&a=188312

Why retrieve top 5 documents instead of only top 1 in RAG?

Because the answer isn't always in one document.

Sometimes:

- One document has the direct answer
- Another adds an important condition
- The top result is close but not sufficient
- The second or third result is actually more useful

Then the LLM can use the retrieved context to produce a better answer

Top 5 is not a magic number; you can choose this number based on your dataset.

You choose it and evaluate the results to make a final decision:

- Maybe top 3 is enough.
- Maybe top 10 improves recall.
- Maybe too many documents add noise.

The important part here is to treat retrieval as something you measure and evaluate.

-> Use my workshop recording to learn how vector search fits into RAG: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BC3NsRUNEIg&t=714s

## 2026-05-29T14:00:07.376Z

- ID: `9164544`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2060360539516420522

Your first RAG implementation should be easy to replace.

In my recent workshop, we started with a modular setup:

1. Search
2. Build prompt
3. Call LLM

Then we moved the logic into reusable components.

It's important because the first version is rarely the final version.

You may start with:

- MinSearch and later move to SQLite search, Elasticsearch, Postgres, or vector search.
- OpenAI and later try Anthropic, Gemini, or a local model.
- A notebook and later move the logic into a service.

If everything is mixed together, every change becomes painful.

A better pattern is to keep dependencies explicit: the search index, the LLM client, the prompt template, the course filter, and the model name.

Then replacing one part does not require rewriting the whole assistant.

Check out my workshop recording to build your first RAG application: https://www.youtube.com/live/KSItlTAsMsk?si=xQDsAgtnzB9wAC4-

## 2026-05-21T12:40:39.181Z

- ID: `9190565`
- Platform: -
- Status: draft
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://typefully.com/?d=9190565&a=188312

Don't start a RAG project with vector search by default.

Start with a text search.

It is simpler:

- No embedding model
- No vector database
- No extra query-time encoding step
- Fewer dependencies
- Easier debugging

Vector search is useful when users ask the same thing with different words.

For example:

"Can I still join the course?" or "Can I still enroll in the program?"

These are semantically close, but a lexical search may miss the connection if the words don't overlap.

That's where embeddings help, but they also add overhead.

You need to encode the dataset, store the vectors, encode each query, choose a search backend, and evaluate whether the improvement is worth it.

My rule:

1. Build version 1 with text search
2. Then evaluate
- If vector search gives a meaningful improvement, add it.
- If the improvement is marginal, the complexity may not be worth it.

-> Use my workshop recording to learn how vector search fits into RAG: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BC3NsRUNEIg&t=714s

- ID: `9176255`

A basic RAG application is just three steps.

1. Search: Find documents that are likely to contain the answer.

2. Build prompt: Turn the user question and search results into context the model can use.

3. Call the LLM: Generate the final answer.

That’s enough for the first version.

In my recent workshop, we built this with a course FAQ assistant. A student asks a question, search returns relevant FAQ entries, we build a prompt with the question and context, and the LLM answers.

The useful part of this structure is that every step is visible.

You can inspect search results.
You can print the prompt.
You can change the model.
You can improve retrieval without touching the LLM call.

Before adding frameworks, agents, or orchestration, make sure these three functions work.

A simple RAG pipeline is much easier to debug when you can see each step.

Check out my workshop recording to build your first RAG application: https://www.youtube.com/live/KSItlTAsMsk?si=xQDsAgtnzB9wAC4-

- ID: `9164546`
- Platform: -

- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2058911128286269612

Most AI engineers should learn RAG before fine-tuning.

Both approaches can help with domain-specific knowledge, but they work differently.

With fine-tuning, you change the model weights.

With RAG, you keep the model as a black box and give it relevant context at request time.

For many applications, RAG is the more practical first step.

1. It is easier to update. If your knowledge base changes, you update the documents or the index. You don’t retrain the model.

2. It works with different LLM providers. The knowledge layer is separate from the model.

3. It is also easier to debug. You can inspect retrieved documents, prompts, answers, and failure modes.

Fine-tuning has valid use cases, but it requires more infrastructure, more specialized tooling, and more careful evaluation.

My default recommendation: start with RAG.

Use fine-tuning only when you have a clear reason that retrieval and prompting cannot solve.

Use my workshop recording to build your first RAG application: https://www.youtube.com/live/KSItlTAsMsk?si=xQDsAgtnzB9wAC4-

## 2026-05-21T14:01:09.122Z

- ID: `9164538`

- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2057461696256753794

RAG demos often look simple because the data is already clean.

In my recent workshop, I used FAQ data from DataTalksClub courses. The data was already available via a JSON endpoint, so we could load, index, and search it.

In real projects, this part is usually not smooth.

You may need to:

- Parse documents
- Scrape pages
- Clean HTML
- Normalize fields
- Remove duplicates
- Add metadata
- Decide what should be searchable and filterable

If the data is messy, the LLM can still produce fluent answers. But the answers may be incomplete, outdated, or wrong.

For many RAG systems, improving the data preparation step gives more value than changing the prompt.

Check out my workshop recording to build your first RAG application: https://www.youtube.com/live/KSItlTAsMsk?si=xQDsAgtnzB9wAC4-

Want to go from LLM basics to a production-ready AI assistant in 10 weeks? Join my free LLM Zoomcamp that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

## 2026-05-20T14:01:11.482Z

- ID: `9164500`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2057099318142894437

A plain LLM can give a helpful answer and still be wrong for your application.

In the workshop, we used a simple student question:

"I just discovered the course. Can I still join?"

When we sent it directly to the LLM, it gave a generic answer: if enrollment is still open, you can usually join.

That sounds reasonable, but it doesn't know our actual course policy.

Then we added the FAQ context and sent the question along with it.

The answer became specific: yes, you can join, but if you want a certificate, you need to submit your project while submissions are open.

That's the basic idea of RAG.

- The model handles generation.
- Search handles access to your knowledge base.
- The prompt connects the user's question with the retrieved context.

Use my workshop recording to build your first RAG application: https://www.youtube.com/live/KSItlTAsMsk?si=xQDsAgtnzB9wAC4-

Want to go from LLM basics to a production-ready AI assistant in 10 weeks? Join my free LLM Zoomcamp that starts on June 8: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/llm-zoomcamp

RAG systems are hard to evaluate by manual testing alone.

A few examples may look good, but they don't tell you whether:

- Retrieval finds the right documents
- Ranking is improving or getting worse
- Generated answers are faithful
- One model is actually better than another
- Changes introduce regressions

Nice to see ML Zoomcamp ranked #1 in @IAmMarinaWyss's review of machine learning courses.

She highlighted that ML Zoomcamp covers both theory and practice in one program:

- Decision trees and ensemble learning

- Model persistence

- Docker

- Kubernetes

One principle I strongly believe in and apply in my courses is this: we teach concepts, not tools.

Yes, each course uses specific technologies, but that doesn't mean you're locked into them.

In a recent Q&A about the upcoming Data Engineering Zoomcamp, I shared that the course is designed to teach:

🔸 What workflow orchestration is
🔸 Why data warehousing functions as it does
🔸 How batch and streaming processing differ
🔸 How analytics engineering integrates into the data stack

The tools are just concrete examples.

Prefer Airflow instead of Kestra?
Snowflake or ClickHouse instead of BigQuery?

Totally fine.

Once you understand the underlying concepts, swapping tools becomes a practical decision.

That's also why the course is structured around clear abstractions, transferable mental models, and real-world data engineering workflows.

Tools change, but concepts stay.

Data Engineering Zoomcamp is our free data engineering course. It starts on January 12, 2026.

Register here to join the cohort and receive your certificate: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp

A few people asked how AI Shipping Labs fits with @DataTalksClub.

Short answer: they're built for different stages.

@DataTalksClub is where you learn the fundamentals. Zoomcamps, free courses, recorded talks. Open access, self-paced, thousands of people learning together.

AI Shipping Labs is where you ship.

It's for people who've learned the theory and need accountability, feedback, and structure to turn that knowledge into working projects.

Smaller groups. Weekly sprints. Direct access. Live workshops where you build, not just watch.

Both will continue. They serve different needs at different points in your learning path.

If you're starting out or prefer self-paced learning, stick with the free content. It's not changing.

If you're ready to build and want hands-on support to actually ship, that's what Labs is for.

No pressure to move from one to the other. Pick what fits where you are now.

In the Data Engineering Zoomcamp, we begin Module 5: Data Platforms.

It teaches how modern data platforms manage the full data lifecycle, from ingestion and transformation to orchestration, data quality, and metadata.

In this module, we use @getbruin as a practical example of a unified data platform and build an end-to-end pipeline on NYC taxi data.

You'll cover:

- Ingestion with Bruin CLI
- SQL and Python transformations
- Three-layer architecture: ingestion -> staging -> reporting
- YAML-based pipeline configuration
- Orchestration, scheduling, and variables
- Data quality checks
- Governance and visibility with Bruin Cloud
- Using Bruin MCP inside your development workflow

The goal is to see how ingestion, transformations, orchestration, and quality checks fit together in a production-style workflow.

Learn here: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp/tree/main/05-data-platforms

Why are entry-level data and software engineering roles so hard to get right now?

AI is not the only reason, but it has changed hiring incentives.

1. Companies are under pressure

Slower markets, tighter budgets, fewer hires.

2. AI boosts senior productivity

One experienced engineer with AI tools can now do work that used to require a small team. From a company’s perspective, prompting AI is often cheaper than onboarding and managing juniors.

3. Hiring shifts as a result

Many teams prefer one strong engineer + AI over several junior roles. Entry-level openings shrink, competition explodes.

This does not mean learning data engineering, SQL, or software engineering is useless.

And AI doesn't replace skills. It raises expectations for how quickly you apply them.

For juniors, knowing the fundamentals is no longer enough on its own.

You're expected to:

🔸 Use AI to read and understand unfamiliar code
🔸 Iterate faster
🔸 Debug and reason about systems more efficiently

If you want to build these skills in practice, our Data Engineering Zoomcamp is free and open: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp

- URL: https://typefully.com/t/mv7LR1B

Data Analyst vs. Data Engineer vs. Analytics Engineer: What's the Difference?

These roles often get mixed up, especially early in a data career.

Here's a practical way to think about them, based on how teams work:

1. Data Analysts

- Focus on what's happening in the data
- Build reports, dashboards, and analyses
- Translate numbers into insights
- Usually closest to business and product

2. Data Engineers

- Make data usable and reliable
- Build pipelines to ingest, clean, and store raw data
- Maintain data warehouses separate from production systems
- Enable analysts, data scientists, and ML engineers to do their work

3. Analytics Engineers

- Sit between analysis and engineering
- Turn raw data into clean, well-modeled tables
- Less infrastructure-heavy than data engineers
- Less business-facing than analysts

If you want to start or switch into data engineering, we offer a free, hands-on course: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp

Data Analyst vs. Data Engineer vs. Analytics Engineer: What's the Difference?

These roles often get mixed up, especially early in a data career.

Here's a practical way to think about them, based on how real teams work:

1. Data Analysts

- Focus on what's happening in the data
- Build reports, dashboards, and analyses
- Translate numbers into insights
- Usually closest to business and product

2. Data Engineers

- Make data usable and reliable
- Build pipelines to ingest, clean, and store raw data
- Maintain data warehouses separate from production systems
- Enable analysts, data scientists, and ML engineers to do their work

3. Analytics Engineers

- Sit between analysis and engineering
- Turn raw data into clean, well-modeled tables
- Less infrastructure-heavy than data engineers
- Less business-facing than analysts

If you want to start or switch into data engineering, we offer a free, hands-on course: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp

- ID: `7633075`

CSV files lack a schema.

When you load a CSV with Pandas, it must infer column types from the data, often leading to issues:

🔸 Integer columns become floats due to missing values
🔸 Datetime columns remain as strings
🔸 You receive warnings that are easy to overlook but challenging to debug later

In my Docker for Data Engineering workshop, I demonstrated a straightforward solution: explicitly define the schema.

Instead of relying on inference, provide a dtype mapping when reading the CSV:

🔸 IDs are read as integers
🔸 Timestamps are parsed as datetimes
🔸 Missing values are handled intentionally

This is crucial because type issues often arise much later:

🔸 When writing to a database
🔸 When switching from Pandas to Spark
🔸 When performing aggregations or joins

If you missed the workshop, here's the recording: https://www.youtube.com/live/lP8xXebHmuE?si=SrME-d1CtPqjnXGu

It will be used to update the Docker module in our free Data Engineering Zoomcamp course.

Register for the zoomcamp here: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp

Why are entry-level data and software engineering roles so hard to get right now?

AI is not the only reason, but it has changed hiring incentives.

1. Companies are under pressure

Slower markets, tighter budgets, fewer hires.

2. AI boosts senior productivity

One experienced engineer with AI tools can now do work that used to require a small team. From a company’s perspective, prompting AI is often cheaper than onboarding and managing juniors.

3. Hiring shifts as a result

Many teams prefer one strong engineer + AI over several junior roles. Entry-level openings shrink, competition explodes.

This does not mean learning data engineering, SQL, or software engineering is useless.

And AI doesn't replace skills. It raises expectations for how quickly you apply them.

For juniors, knowing the fundamentals is no longer enough on its own.

You're expected to:

🔸 Use AI to read and understand unfamiliar code
🔸 Iterate faster
🔸 Debug and reason about systems more efficiently

If you want to build these skills in practice, our Data Engineering Zoomcamp is free and open: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp

A common question I receive is: Should I take the Data Engineering Zoomcamp? Do I know enough, or should I upskill first?

My advice is straightforward: use the Docker workshop as a diagnostic test.

I recently conducted a Docker workshop, which will replace the Docker section of the course. It will eventually become the first module, but you can access it now.

If you have time and plan to take the course, start with this workshop.

The aim is to pinpoint your knowledge gaps.

As you progress through it, consider these questions:

🔸 Is using the command line challenging?
🔸 Do you feel lost when something breaks?
🔸 Are the basic concepts unfamiliar to you?

If you answer yes to any of these, it's a valuable signal.

You have time before the course starts, and this highlights exactly what you need to practice:

If the command line is difficult, practice working in the terminal. Or if Python is a weakness, focus on improving your Python fundamentals.

The Docker workshop helps you identify areas to work on.

Watch it here: https://www.youtube.com/live/lP8xXebHmuE?si=SrME-d1CtPqjnXGu

One principle I strongly believe in and apply in my courses is this: we teach concepts, not tools.

Yes, each course uses specific technologies, but that doesn't mean you're locked into them.

In a recent Q&A about the upcoming Data Engineering Zoomcamp, I shared that the course is designed to teach:

🔸 What workflow orchestration is
🔸 Why data warehousing functions as it does
🔸 How batch and streaming processing differ
🔸 How analytics engineering integrates into the data stack

The tools are just concrete examples.

Prefer Airflow instead of Kestra?
Snowflake or ClickHouse instead of BigQuery?

Totally fine.

Once you understand the underlying concepts, swapping tools becomes a practical decision.

That's also why the course is structured around clear abstractions, transferable mental models, and real-world data engineering workflows.

Tools change, but concepts stay.

Data Engineering Zoomcamp is our free data engineering course. It starts on January 12, 2026.

- ID: `7631961`

- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2008916653825028508

Docker containers are stateless by default.

Anything you create inside a container (files, data, outputs) disappears as soon as the container stops.

So how do we work with code and data?

The answer: volume mapping.

In Docker, you can mount a directory from your local machine into the container.

This way:

🔸 Your files live on your laptop
🔸 The container can read and modify them
🔸 Restarting or deleting the container doesn't delete your work

You run code in an isolated environment, but the data persists outside of it.

Using volume mapping is part of my Docker for Data Engineering workshop.

Watch it here: https://www.youtube.com/live/lP8xXebHmuE?si=SrME-d1CtPqjnXGu

The recording is now part of the Data Engineering Zoomcamp, which starts on January 12, 2026.

Join the course to learn Docker the way it's used in real data projects: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp

- ID: `7650176`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2006742311729012854

The job market is tough right now, especially for entry-level roles.

Companies can often get the productivity of several junior engineers from one experienced hire.

That means more competition and higher expectations.

So the question I hear most often is: How do I stand out?

My answer is very practical: Don't build generic projects. Build domain-specific ones.

Here's the strategy I recommend:

1. Start by looking at open positions on LinkedIn. Try to understand which domains companies work in: e-commerce, finance, healthcare, manufacturing, and so on. Pick a domain you’re actually interested in.

2. Then do some research:

🔸 read tech blogs from companies hiring in that domain
🔸 look at the kinds of data problems they describe
🔸 streaming systems, event processing, analytics pipelines, etc.

3. Next step: replicate those problems on a smaller scale.

If you can find a similar dataset, use it. If not, generate one.

Then try to solve the same type of problem, just simplified.

This does a few important things:

🔸 you understand what problems exist in that domain
🔸 you already get exposure before being hired
🔸 you stand out from candidates who only have generic projects

4. You can mention this work in your cover letter.

You can talk about it during interviews.

When they ask questions, you have something concrete and relevant to discuss.

Do this for a couple of projects, and that's usually enough to make a difference.

5. This is also why, in my courses, we focus not just on foundations, but on projects.

With this approach, you can already build one strong project for your portfolio, start applying, and continue adding more over time.

We're launching Data Engineering Zoomcamp 2026 on January 12, 2026.

Register here: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp

- ID: `7631540`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/2007104693559050432

One important Docker concept that often causes confusion: containers are stateless.

A Docker image is a snapshot of an operating system.

Every time you run docker run, Docker creates a new container from that snapshot.

What this means in practice:

🔸 You can create files inside a container
🔸 You can install packages
🔸 You can modify the filesystem

But as soon as you exit the container, all those changes are gone.

When you start the container again, you're back to exactly what's defined in the image.

It's the core idea behind Docker for:

🔸 reproducibility
🔸 clean environments
🔸 predictable behavior

If you want data to persist, you need to be explicit about it (volumes, mounted paths, databases).

I recently walked through this step-by-step in my Docker for Data Engineering workshop, using PostgreSQL, Docker Compose, and real workflows.

Watch it here: https://www.youtube.com/live/lP8xXebHmuE?si=SrME-d1CtPqjnXGu

It will be used to update the Docker module of the Data Engineering Zoomcamp, our free data engineering course. It starts on January 12, 2026.

Register here: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/data-engineering-zoomcamp

We frequently receive questions about the Data Engineering Zoomcamp.

Here are 5 main Q&As + how you can ask me your questions live:

1. How much time per week should I allocate to stay on track?

Estimated weekly workload based on homework statistics: approximately 10 hours per week. Beginners might need closer to 15-20 hours, especially for Module 1 (Docker), which is the most challenging.

2. What should I prepare before the course begins?

Key prerequisites:

- Install Docker early, especially if you're on Windows
- Create a GitHub account
- Join Slack
- Set up GCP
- Have a basic understanding of Python and SQL

3. Can I land a data engineering job after the Zoomcamp?

Yes, many do!

The course teaches the key 20% of skills for 80% of real-world tasks.

You'll need a strong project and some self-study, but it's a great start for junior or mid-level roles.

4. Is the course suitable for beginners or recent graduates?

It depends.

Comfort with basic programming and the terminal is needed.
SQL and Python basics are a must.

Aspiring data engineers can start here.
For general software roles, it might be wise to wait.

5. What's new in this cohort?

The Docker module is updated. It now guides you through a full workflow with Docker, PostgreSQL, pgAdmin, and Docker Compose. Learn to run and link multiple services effortlessly.

Want to ask your question and get an answer from me? Join me for a live Q&A on Monday, December 15.

Register here: https://luma.com/9nefsjwl

We frequently receive questions about the Data Engineering Zoomcamp.

Here are 5 main Q&As + how you can ask me your questions live:

1. How much time per week should I allocate to stay on track?

Estimated weekly workload based on homework statistics: approximately 10 hours per week. Beginners might need closer to 15-20 hours, especially for Module 1 (Docker), which is the most challenging.

2. What should I prepare before the course begins?

Key prerequisites:

- Install Docker early, especially if you're on Windows
- Create a GitHub account
- Join Slack
- Set up GCP
- Have a basic understanding of Python (loops, functions, conditionals) and SQL (SELECT, JOIN, GROUP BY, ORDER BY)

3. Can I get a data engineering job after completing the Zoomcamp?

Yes, many participants do. The course covers the essential 20% of skills needed for 80% of real-world applications. You will still need a solid project and some additional self-study, but it's enough to apply for junior or mid-level roles, depending on your background.

4. Is the course suitable for beginners or recent graduates?

It depends. You need to be comfortable with basic programming and not afraid of using the terminal. SQL and Python fundamentals are required. If you want to become a data engineer, the course is suitable even as a first step. If you're aiming for general software roles, you might want to wait.

5. What’s new or different in this cohort?

I updated the Docker module. It covers a complete workflow using Docker, PostgreSQL, pgAdmin, and Docker Compose, demonstrating how to run and connect multiple services with minimal setup.

Want to ask your question and get an answer from me? Join me for a live Q&A on Monday, December 15.

Register here: https://luma.com/9nefsjwl

3 parts of the MCP architecture: host, client, and server

🧵

1. MCP Host: the environment where your AI operates.

This could be your Claude UI, VS Code with Copilot, or Cursor—essentially any interface that can coordinate LLM actions.

2. MCP Client: the code that mediates between the host and the server.

It sends requests, receives responses, and manages communication according to the MCP standard.

3. MCP Server: the bridge to real-world data and tools.

Servers expose your files, Git repositories, Slack messages, or other applications through standardized connectors. Each tool (for example, a Slack MCP server) implements its own logic behind a consistent API.

4. This setup lets developers create LLM integrations that are:

🔸 Predictable: Uniform communication between clients and servers
🔸 Portable: Connects to various tools without altering code
🔸 Debuggable: Uses JSON so you can inspect every request and response

This explanation is based on Bhavani Ravi's workshop on MCP, where she explained how it connects LLMs with real-world tools, data systems, and developer workflows.

If you'd like to watch the full session, you can find the recording here: https://youtube.com/live/0IhZdcjddo4

If you want to learn using MCP in your projects, join the AI Dev Tools Zoomcamp – a free course on using AI tools for devs: https://datatalks.club/courses/ai-dev-tools-zoomcamp/

## 2025-11-21T08:00:39.085Z

- ID: `7227843`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp, free course
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/1991778711067377963

MCP is built on key components known as primitives.

These define how clients and servers communicate, share data, and perform actions through a common language.

MCP servers introduce three main primitives:

1. Tools ➡️ Actions that perform tasks

Examples include generating a summary or fetching the weather. Each tool represents an operation the AI can activate.

2. Resources ➡️ Information sources

They don't perform actions but return data—such as files, configuration details, or other structured content that the AI can utilize.

3. Prompts ➡️ Text-generating functions

These return a string, often a reusable prompt template or instruction for the model.

This overview is based on Bhavani Ravi’s workshop on MCP, where she explained how it connects LLMs with real-world tools, data systems, and developer workflows.

If you'd like to watch the full session, you can find the recording here: https://youtube.com/live/0IhZdcjddo4

If you want to learn using MCP in your projects, join the AI Dev Tools Zoomcamp – a free course on using AI tools for devs: https://datatalks.club/courses/ai-dev-tools-zoomcamp/

- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/1988879577939325127

What is MCP? Why does it matter for developers?

🧵

Before MCP, integrating LLMs with company tools or data was fragmented.

Each integration required custom connectors and manual upkeep.

This created a significant burden for teams managing many integrations.

MCP, introduced by Anthropic, transforms this landscape. It provides a common language for integrations.

Instead of developers creating unique adapters, MCP establishes a shared protocol.

This enables communication among LLMs, IDEs, and apps.

This insight is from Bhavani Ravi's workshop on MCP.

She discussed how it connects LLMs with real-world tools, data systems, and developer workflows.

If you'd like to see the full session, you can watch the recording here: https://youtube.com/live/0IhZdcjddo4

If you want to learn using MCP in your projects, join the AI Dev Tools Zoomcamp – a free course on using AI tools for devs: https://datatalks.club/courses/ai-dev-tools-zoomcamp/

We've reached the midterm projects at ML Zoomcamp!

Now, it's time to transition from notebooks to real, deployable machine learning applications.

Here's a quick list of 6 key tips for a successful submission:

1️⃣ Choose your problem wisely

Pick a project you're passionate about: something intriguing enough to keep you engaged in debugging and refining for two weeks.

2️⃣ Frame it clearly

Explain what you're predicting, why it matters, and how you'll measure success (e.g., accuracy, AUC, RMSE).

3️⃣ Keep data handling clean

Document your data sources, check for quality, and ensure your preprocessing steps are reproducible.

4️⃣ Model smartly

Begin with a baseline model, test at least three models, use cross-validation, and avoid data leakage.

5️⃣ Package it properly

Transition from notebook ➡️ scripts ➡️ web service ➡️ Docker.

A functional POST /predict endpoint and a Dockerfile are required.

6️⃣ Bonus points

Deploy to the cloud, document everything, and share your progress publicly.

💡 Pro tip: Clone your repo fresh before submission.

If it runs smoothly for you, it will run smoothly for reviewers.

Good luck, and remember: Deployment always takes longer than you expect so start early!

Submit your project here: https://courses.datatalks.club/ml-zoomcamp-2025/project/midterm

Deadline: 18 November 2025, 00:00 CET

A functional POST /predict endpoint and a Dockerfile are required.

6️⃣ Bonus points

Deploy to the cloud, document everything, and share your progress publicly.

💡 Pro tip: Clone your repo fresh before submission.

If it runs smoothly for you, it will run smoothly for reviewers.

Good luck, and remember: Deployment always takes longer than you expect so start early!

Submit your project here: https://courses.datatalks.club/ml-zoomcamp-2025/project/midterm

Deadline: 18 November 2025, 00:00 CET

10 most popular questions about AI Dev Tools summarized:

1. What is the purpose of the AI Dev Tools Zoomcamp?

Help developers become more productive and effective by using modern AI tools to write code faster.

2. Who is the course for?

The course is designed for anyone who writes code, including MLOps engineers, ML engineers, AI engineers, and software developers. It focuses on software development workflows rather than data-specific tasks.

3. What are the course prerequisites?

Basic programming and command-line knowledge are recommended.

4. What topics are covered?

🔸 Overview of chatbots, coding assistants, and AI agents
🔸 Building an end-to-end project (frontend + backend + database)
🔸 Understanding AI agents and MCP
🔸 Creating a custom coding agent
🔸 Using AI for DevOps automation with tools like n8n

5. Does the course require any paid LLM subscription?

🔸 Module 1: Free (works with free tools like Groq or Gemini)
🔸 Module 2: Likely requires Cursor ($20/mo) or GitHub Copilot ($10/mo)
🔸 Module 4: Small API deposit (~$5) for OpenAI or Anthropic

6. Is the course free?

Yes. All course materials and participation are free (as with other DataTalksClub courses).

7. When does it start?

November 18, 2025 (Tuesday).

8. What platforms are used?

🔸 YouTube for video lectures
🔸 Slack for community and Q&A
🔸 Telegram for announcements

9. How does the developer workflow change with AI tools?

Developers move from primarily writing code to reading, reviewing, and improving AI-generated code.

10. Can I take the course after the cohort ends?

Yes, materials are available for self-paced learning.

But self-paced learning does not include homework submissions, project evaluations, and the opportunity to earn a certificate.

You can find answers to other questions in the recording of my live Q&A: https://youtube.com/live/sUwrCnP2iGU

Setting up transfer learning? Your first big challenge is data prep, and not all frameworks handle it the same way.

With PyTorch, you get the granularity you need to:

🔸 Iterating through files and directories
🔸 Opening and reading images
🔸 Applying transformations (normalization, resizing, augmentation)

This level of control is perfect for intricate or custom data setups, but it requires more coding and debugging.

Conversely, Keras provides high-level utilities that automate iteration, batching, and transformations. This is ideal for quick experimentation.

So choose your framework based on your needs.

If you're interested in learning both tools, check out the 8th module of our free ML Zoomcamp: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/machine-learning-zoomcamp/tree/master/08-deep-learning

Also, watch my PyTorch workshop: https://www.youtube.com/live/Ne25VujHRLA?si=H9pshyOlc4TVtg7L

People in the comments are asking: "What is the AI Dev Tools Zoomcamp?"

This course is designed for developers or anyone working with code who wants to integrate AI tools into their workflows. It helps automate tasks, allowing you to spend more time on building.

Over 6 modules, we cover key use cases and topics, finalizing the course with a project where you integrate AI.

Learn about the main types of AI tools for coding: chatbots, IDEs, project bootstrappers, and agents.

2️⃣ Module 2

Try to build a simple game in React/TS, define an API with OpenAPI, generate FastAPI server from OpenAPI specs, add CI/CD, deploy the application

3️⃣ Module 3

Add tools to your AI assistants using Model-Context Protocol (MCP) to do things like repo triage, PR summarization, scripted edits, and data queries

4️⃣ Module 4

Build your first AI coding agent that can scaffold and extend projects, explore multiple agent orchestration frameworks

5️⃣ Module 5

Learn to use AI for testing, CI/CD and DevOps

6️⃣ Module 6

Understand where to automate vs. code and explore with low-code and no-code AI (n8n)

7️⃣ Course Project
Build a complete application using AI tools throughout the entire development lifecycle

Join the course here: https://datatalks.club/courses/ai-dev-tools-zoomcamp/

5 Kubernetes core concepts

🧵

1. Nodes

Machines, whether virtual or physical, that run your workloads.

2. Pods

The smallest deployable unit—typically a single containerized application instance.

3. Deployments

Manage multiple pods to ensure high availability.

4. Services

Act as load balancers, distributing traffic across replicas.

5. HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler)

Dynamically scales pods based on the workload.

6. It's complex at first, but once mastered, Kubernetes becomes a powerful ally for production-grade AI systems.

We learn how to work with Kubernetes in one of the updated modules of ML Zoomcamp 2025.

The course is currently live, and we'll soon move to deployment modules.

You can join us to learn together: https://datatalks.club/courses/ml-zoomcamp/

## 2025-10-23T07:00:30.885Z

- ID: `6951674`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://x.com/Al_Grigor/status/1981254328980820113

Many DevOps engineers are transitioning into MLOps or AI Engineering.

The great news is you don't need deep ML theory to get started.

If you already know CI/CD, Docker, and Kubernetes, you're closer than you think.

Here's how these roles differ 👇

🔸 MLOps = operational excellence for ML
🔸 ML Engineering = model development
🔸 AI Engineering = integrating intelligent systems into production

1. MLOps

Focus: Infrastructure & reliability for ML systems.

You'll work on:

- Managing Kubernetes clusters
- Automating model deployments & monitoring pipelines
- Troubleshooting inference issues

🧠 ML knowledge needed: Just enough to understand what the model is doing.

2. ML Engineering

Focus: Building and refining models before deployment.

You'll need to know:

- Regression
- Model evaluation metrics
- Algorithms like XGBoost
- Basics of deep learning

🧠 ML knowledge needed: Intermediate, the level covered in most applied ML courses.

3. AI Engineering (today)

Focus: Integrating AI into products

Often involves calling APIs (like OpenAI) instead of training models

Similar to data and backend engineering, ensuring smooth data flow and system stability.

ML Zoomcamp provides the foundation you need for a successful transition. We're midway through the course, so there's still time to catch up, submit all projects, and earn your certificate.

You can join the 2025 cohort here: https://datatalks.club/courses/ml-zoomcamp/

## 2025-10-04T07:00:18.915Z

- ID: `6692398`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://typefully.com/t/Fb3CKNT

A lot of people ask whether “learning in public” is worth it.
Here’s a concrete story from Pastor Soto that shows how it pays off.

When Pastor Soto joined ML Zoomcamp, he didn't even have a LinkedIn account.

1/6

A medical student by background, he was curious about machine learning and wanted to see how far he could go.

He started sharing his progress publicly.

Initially, it was just about posting assignments to earn leaderboard points.

2/6

But soon, he realized that explaining what he was learning helped him:

🔸 Learn faster
🔸 Build confidence
🔸 And, most importantly, get noticed

3/6

A few months later, recruiters started reaching out. One was from Meta. Another from DeepLearning AI.

All because of the posts he was sharing.

4/6

He didn't land the job at Meta (yet), but that wasn’t the point. The real win was:

🔸 Experiencing real interviews early
🔸 Growing his reputation as a practitioner
🔸 Turning course projects into a lasting portfolio

5/6

Today, Pastor is a Machine Learning Engineer and a mentor at DeepLearning AI.

So if you're learning ML, data, or AI: don't keep it to yourself.

Start small. Share one concept, one project, one lesson.

6/6

## 2025-09-24T07:00:14.193Z

- ID: `6692220`
- Platform: -
- Status: published
- Matched terms: zoomcamp
- URL: https://typefully.com/t/cuk2TY3

What's the real value of learning in public?

Let me share a story from ML Zoomcamp.

During the course, Daniel Egbo shared his progress openly on LinkedIn:

🔸 the projects he was building
🔸 the certificate he earned
🔸 his reflections along the way

1/4

That visibility led to opportunities:

A company reached out with a project idea.

Later, someone contacted him to collaborate on an ML project.

The project involved deploying models on Intel’s OpenVINO toolkit and testing them on Intel’s cloud infrastructure.

2/4

Daniel described it as challenging but also a lot of fun.

He also shared his view on learning in public:

Learning in public doesn't have to be only about praise or recognition. It can provide feedback from the community that helps you grow faster than working in isolation.

3/4

So if you’re learning ML, data science, or AI:

Post your progress. Share your work. Show what you're building.

You never know what doors it might open.

4/4

Thinking about ML Zoomcamp?

Alexander Daniel Rios shared his story of building his final project on cancer prediction using microscope images.

1/5

How it’s packaged:

You’ve asked us: “Is ML Zoomcamp practical? Will I actually build something real?”

Here’s an answer from one of the course graduates, Serena Haidar, who has built an end-to-end waste image classification system.

Serena just published a case study from her final project.

It shows her path from a notebook to a small, working app:

🔸 Task: Waste image classification (8 classes + biodegradability)
🔸 Data: ~15,200 images
🔸 Model: Transfer learning with Xception
🔸 Result: 93.3% test accuracy
🔸 Delivery: Flask API, packaged with Docker

During the course, you apply concepts to an end-to-end project: from data to model selection and evaluation to deployment.

We frequently receive questions about ML Zoomcamp.

Here are some of the main Q&As + how you can ask me your questions live:

1. How is the course structured?

🔸 4 months: pre-recorded videos + weekly live office hours
🔸 Hands-on projects & vibrant Slack community
🔸 Time commitment: ~10 hours/week

2. How can I start learning?

🔸 Self-paced: Start any time
🔸 Live cohort: Starts on Sept 15, 2025. Learn with peers & earn a certificate.

3. What’s included in the live cohort?

🔸 Regular live office hours with instructors
🔸 Structured deadlines & peer support
🔸 Certificate upon completion
🔸 Access to all recordings (even if you join late!)

4. How do I get certified?

🔸 Enroll in the live cohort
🔸 Complete 2 of 3 projects (Midterm + Capstone)
🔸 Review 3 peers' projects by the deadline

5. Can I join after the start date?

Yes! All materials remain accessible, just catch up on any missed homework.

Got other questions?

Join a live Q&A with me on Tuesday, August 19, 5:00 PM CET.

Register here: 
https://lu.ma/93concgq

We’re excited to share the results from our LLM Zoomcamp 2024 Competition, where participants applied LLMs to solve high-school math problems.

Here’s what you’ll find in the article:

🔸 An overview of the competition and the dataset participants worked with

🔸 Four winning solutions, each taking a unique approach:

1. Artur G. combined code generation with chain-of-thought reasoning to solve problems step-by-step.

2. Blaqadonis used an agent-based system, leveraging LangGraph for modular problem-solving.

3. Vladyslav Khaitov employed a hybrid model approach, mixing open-source and closed-source models for optimal performance.

4. Slava Shen integrated data-driven logic to blend results and improve accuracy.

Explore these approaches and gain insights into LLM strategies like prompt engineering and agentic systems.

Read the full article to learn more and prepare for your next competition! https://datatalks.club/blog/winning-solutions-from-llm-zoomcamp-2024-competition.html

In Module 3 of the ML Zoomcamp, we focus on predicting customer churn.

Here’s our approach:

🔸 Data Collection: Gather customer data
🔸 Data Preparation: Clean and encode data
🔸 Feature Engineering: Identify key churn factors
🔸 Validation: Ensure strong model generalization
🔸 Model Training: Use logistic regression for predictions
🔸 Model Interpretation: Analyze churn reasons
🔸 Actionable Insights: Target high-risk customers with promotions to reduce churn

Churn prediction helps companies send targeted offers like discounts or promotions to reduce churn and retain customers.

💡 Want to learn how to implement this model? Join us here: https://github.com/DataTalksClub/machine-learning-zoomcamp/tree/master/03-classification
